(2.36p)

2.3
Twisted partition function of the
O(n)
CFT

Definition and character decomposition

Let us define the twisted partition function of the
O(n)
CFT on a torus of modulus
\tau
as

\displaystyle Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}}\left(e^{2\pi i\tau(L_{0}-\frac{c}{24})}e^{2\pi i\bar{\tau}(\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24})}\cdot g\right)\ ,

(2.37)

where the twist factor is an element
g\in O(n)
, the space of states
\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}
is given by (
2.25
), and
L_{0},\bar{L}_{0}
are elements of the conformal algebra
\mathfrak{C}_{c}
. The decomposition (
2.20
) of the space of states
\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}
into representations of
O(n)
and
\mathfrak{C}_{c}
leads to a decomposition of the partition function into characters,

\displaystyle Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)=\sum_{s\in 2\mathbb{N}+1}\chi_{\langle 1,s\rangle}(\tau)+\sum_{r\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}^{*}}\sum_{s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}}\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)\chi^{O(n)}_{\Lambda_{(r,s)}}(g)\ .

(2.38)

Here we introduced the characters of representations of
\mathfrak{C}_{c}
,

\displaystyle\chi_{\langle 1,s\rangle}(\tau)=\left|\frac{e^{2\pi i\tau P^{2}_{(r,s)}}-e^{2\pi i\tau P^{2}_{(r,-s)}}}{\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}\qquad,\qquad\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)=\frac{e^{2\pi i\tau P^{2}_{(r,s)}}e^{-2\pi i\bar{\tau}P^{2}_{(r,-s)}}}{|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\ ,

(2.39)

where
\eta(\tau)
is the Dedekind eta function. These are the characters of the representations that appear in Table (
2.2
),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,s\rangle}}\left(e^{2\pi i\tau(L_{0}-\frac{c}{24})}e^{2\pi i\bar{\tau}(\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24})}\right)

\displaystyle=\chi_{\langle 1,s\rangle}(\tau)\ ,

(2.40)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{W}_{(r,s)}}\left(e^{2\pi i\tau(L_{0}-\frac{c}{24})}e^{2\pi i\bar{\tau}(\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24})}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)&\text{if}\quad r\notin\mathbb{Z}^{*}\text{ or }s\notin\mathbb{Z}^{*}\ ,\\
\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)+\chi^{N}_{(r,-s)}(\tau)&\text{if}\quad r,s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\ ,\\
0&\text{if}\quad r,-s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\ .\end{array}\right.

(See
[
11
]
for the determination of the character of the logarithmic representation
\mathcal{W}_{(r,s)}
with
r,s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.)

The modular invariant partition function of
[
3
]
is the special case
Z^{O(n)}(\tau,\text{id})
. In its decomposition into characters, the
O(n)
characters reduce to
\chi^{O(n)}_{\Lambda_{(r,s)}}(\text{id})=\dim^{O(n)}\Lambda_{(r,s)}
. These dimensions are polynomial functions of
n
, for example
\dim^{O(n)}\Lambda_{(1,0)}=\frac{1}{2}(n+2)(n-1)
. Knowing the dimensions helped us guess the conjecture (
2.2
) for
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
in
[
5
]
, although this is not enough for proving the conjecture—a representation is not determined by its dimension.

On the other hand, the character
\chi^{O(n)}_{\Lambda_{(r,s)}}(g)
fully determines the representation
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
. In order to prove the conjecture, it is therefore enough to compute the twisted partition function
Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)
and check that it agrees with (
2.38
). Due to the inclusion of a group element
g
, the proof will only be valid for
n\in\mathbb{N}
. However, just like Newell–Littlewood numbers, the structure of
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
is
n
-independent for
n
generic, and can be accessed from integer values of
n
by taking the limit
n\to\infty
.

Loop representation of the
O(n)
model

For
n
in a rather large region of the complex plane, including for
n\in(-2,2)
, the
O(n)
CFT is the infinite-size limit of the
O(n)
lattice model at the critical coupling constant,
K=K_{c}

[
5
,
7
]
. The simplest version of the lattice model is defined on a hexagonal lattice, in which case
[
2
]

\displaystyle K_{c}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2+\sqrt{2-n}}}\ .

(2.44)

In the loop representation, the
O(n)
model’s partition function can be written as a combination of partition functions of free fields
[
3
]
. We will
now perform a similar calculation in the case of the twisted partition function
Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)
(
2.37
).

In the torus
\mathbb{T}=\frac{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}}
, a loop is topologically characterized by a pair of integers
(m,m^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}=\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T})
, its winding numbers with respect to the two periods of
\mathbb{T}
. A loop with
(m,m^{\prime})=(0,0)
is called a topologically trivial, or contractible loop. The loops of the loop representation are non-self-intersecting, which implies that for any topologically non-trivial loop
m
and
m^{\prime}
are coprime,
\mathrm{gcd}(m,m^{\prime})=1
. As an example, let us draw the loop
(m,m^{\prime})=(3,2)
, together with the lattice
\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
, in the complex plane
\mathbb{C}
, where the torus may be identified with one of the basic parallelograms:

0
m
m^{\prime}

(2.45)

In the loop model, a configuration is a finite set of non-intersecting loops on a lattice. Forbidding intersections implies that only one type of topologically non-trivial loops can exist in a given configuration. For a configuration
\mathcal{C}
, we write

•

N_{0}
the number of contractible loops,

•

N
the number of topologically non-trivial loops,

•

(m,m^{\prime})
the class of the topologically non-trivial loops in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T})
if
N\neq 0
,

•

|A|
the number of lattice edges that belong to the union of all loops in
\mathcal{C}
.

The partition function of the lattice
O(n)
model is

\displaystyle Z^{O(n)}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,g|K)=\sum_{\mathcal{C}}K^{|A|(\mathcal{C})}n^{N_{0}(\mathcal{C})}\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{m(\mathcal{C})}\right)^{N(\mathcal{C})}\ ,

(2.46)

where
K
is the model’s coupling constant. The idea is that each contractible loop has weight
n
, and each topologically non-trivial loop has weight
\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{m}
. Twisting the partition function by
g
indeed amounts to picking a factor
g
whenever a loop crosses the cycle
(0,1)
in one direction, and a factor
g^{-1}
in the other direction. A topologically non-trivial loop therefore comes with the weight
\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{m}=\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{-m}
. This weight reduces to the weight
n
of contractible loops in the case
g=\text{id}
.
Following
[
3
]
, let us relate this partition function to sums over oriented loop configurations. For an oriented loop configuration, we define

•

N_{+},N_{-}
the numbers of topologically non-trivial loops of each orientation, so that
N=N_{+}+N_{-}
,

•

M=m(N_{+}-N_{-})
and
M^{\prime}=m^{\prime}(N_{+}-N_{-})
the algebraic numbers of loops that cross each one of the two basic cycles
(0,1)
and
(1,0)
.

As we will shortly review, it is easy to compute the following sums over oriented loop configurations with fixed numbers
M,M^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle Z^{M,M^{\prime}}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,n|K)=\sum_{\text{oriented }\mathcal{C}|(M(\mathcal{C}),M^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}))=(M,M^{\prime})}K^{|A|(\mathcal{C})}n^{N_{0}(\mathcal{C})}\ .

(2.47)

Let us therefore rewrite
Z^{O(n)}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,g|K)
in terms of these sums. We want to interpret the weight
z=\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{m}
of an unoriented topologically non-trivial loop as a sum over the two possible orientations. A priori, the two orientations can have arbitrary weights
q_{+},q_{-}
such that
q_{+}+q_{-}=z
. However, we also impose

q_{+}q_{-}=1
, so that the weight
q_{+}^{N_{+}}q_{-}^{N_{-}}
of a configuration only depends on the difference
N_{+}-N_{-}
. Using the Chebyshev polynomials (
2.26
), and the property
U_{-d}(z)=U_{d}(z)
which follows from the definition, we have

\displaystyle 2z^{N}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}N_{+},N_{-}\in\mathbb{N}\\
N_{+}+N_{-}=N\end{subarray}}\binom{N}{N_{+}}U_{N_{+}-N_{-}}(z)\underset{N_{+}\equiv N_{-}\bmod 2}{=}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}N_{+},N_{-}\in\mathbb{N}\\
N_{+}+N_{-}=N\end{subarray}}\binom{N}{N_{+}}U_{\frac{1}{2}(N_{+}-N_{-})}(z^{2}-2)\ ,

(2.48)

where the second equality is written for later use, with the help of Eq. (
2.28
).
Since the values of
M,M^{\prime}
determine the difference
N_{+}-N_{-}=\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})
as well as
m=\frac{M}{\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})}
, this allows us to express the
O(n)
model’s partition function in terms of the fixed
M,M^{\prime}
sums
Z^{M,M^{\prime}}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,n|K)
:

\displaystyle Z^{O(n)}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,g|K)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{M,M^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}}Z^{M,M^{\prime}}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,n|K)U_{\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})}\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{\frac{M}{\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})}}\right)\ .

(2.49)

Critical limit

In the critical limit, the sum
Z^{M,M^{\prime}}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,n|K)
tends to the partition function of a compactified free boson, restricted to field configurations of a given topology. The compactification radius of the boson is
\frac{1}{4}\beta^{2}
, where
\beta^{2}
is defined as a function of
n
in Eq. (
1.4
). A field configuration is a map from the torus
\mathbb{T}
to the circle
S^{1}
of radius
\frac{1}{4}\beta^{2}
, and its topology is specified by the images of the basic cycles
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, which we denote as
M,M^{\prime}\in\pi_{1}(S^{1})=\mathbb{Z}
. The Gaussian functional integral over such field configurations yields
[
3
]

\displaystyle Z^{M,M^{\prime}}(\tau,n)=\lim_{\text{critical}}Z^{M,M^{\prime}}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,n|K)=\frac{\beta}{2\sqrt{\Im\tau}|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi\beta^{2}}{4\Im\tau}\left(M^{2}|\tau|^{2}-2MM^{\prime}\Re\tau+M^{\prime 2}\right)}\ .

(2.50)

Let us insert this into Eq. (
2.49
) in order to compute
Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)=\lim_{\text{critical}}Z^{O(n)}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,g|K)
. We start with the term
M=0
. In this case, using Eq. (
1.4
), we find
U_{\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})}\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{\frac{M}{\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})}}\right)=U_{M^{\prime}}(n)=(-1)^{M^{\prime}}\sum_{\pm}e^{\pm\pi i\beta^{2}M^{\prime}}
. Performing a Poisson resummation of the sum over
M^{\prime}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left.Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)\right|_{M=0}=\sum_{s\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1}\left|\frac{e^{2\pi i\tau P_{(1,s)}^{2}}}{\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}=\sum_{s\in 2\mathbb{N}+1}\chi_{\langle 1,s\rangle}(\tau)+\sum_{s\in 2\mathbb{Z}+1}\chi^{N}_{(1,s)}(\tau)\ ,

(2.51)

where the characters of the conformal algebra were defined in Eq. (
2.39
). Next we focus on the terms
M\neq 0
. In this case we write
M^{\prime}=kM+r^{\prime}
with
r^{\prime}\in\{0,1,\dots,M-1\}
, so that
U_{\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})}\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{\frac{M}{\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})}}\right)
does not depend on
k
. We perform a Poisson resummation of the Gaussian sum over
k
, which yields

\left.Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)\right|_{M\neq 0}=\frac{1}{|\eta(\tau)|^{2}}\sum_{M=1}^{\infty}\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{M-1}U_{\mathrm{gcd}(M,r^{\prime})}\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{\frac{M}{\mathrm{gcd}(M,r^{\prime})}}\right)\\
\times\frac{1}{M}\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{2\pi i\frac{\ell r^{\prime}}{M}}e^{-\pi\Im\tau\left(\frac{4}{\beta^{2}}\frac{\ell^{2}}{M^{2}}+\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}M^{2}\right)}e^{2\pi i\ell\Re\tau}\ .

(2.52)

Let us rewrite this in terms of non-diagonal characters of the conformal algebra, while replacing the variables
M,\ell
with
r=\frac{M}{2}
and

s=\frac{2\ell}{M}
. We obtain

\displaystyle\left.Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)\right|_{M\neq 0}=\sum_{r\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}^{*}}\sum_{s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}}\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)\frac{1}{2r}\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{2r-1}e^{\pi ir^{\prime}s}U_{\mathrm{gcd}(2r,r^{\prime})}\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g^{\frac{2r}{\mathrm{gcd}(2r,r^{\prime})}}\right)\ .

(2.53)

Therefore, we have proved that the expression for the twisted partition function
Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)=\left.Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)\right|_{M=0}+\left.Z^{O(n)}(\tau,g)\right|_{M\neq 0}
that we derived from the loop representation of the
O(n)
model, is indeed of the form (
2.38
) predicted by the model’s symmetries, with the
O(n)
characters (
2.31
).

3
Space of states of the Potts CFT

We will now determine the space of states of the Potts CFT, in the same way as we have done for the
O(n)
CFT in Section
2
. The comparison between the two models is often illuminating, and we encourage the reader to keep the
O(n)
CFT in mind. However, it is also possible to read this section independently, and only refer to Section
2
for a few definitions that we will not repeat.

3.1
Representations and characters of the symmetric group

Representations and their tensor products

Irreducible, finite-dimensional representations of the symmetric group
S_{Q}
are parametrized by Young diagrams with
Q
boxes.
To define the representation theory of
S_{Q}
with generic complex values of
Q
, we first have to parametrize these representations in a
Q
-independent way. This is done by distinguishing the first row in the Young diagram from the remaining rows. To
\lambda=[\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}\cdots]
an integer partition, and for integer
Q\geq|\lambda|+\lambda_{1}
, we associate the representation of
S_{Q}
that is parametrized by the Young diagram
[Q-|\lambda|,\lambda]
:

Q-|\lambda|

(3.1)

With this notation, the trivial, one-dimensional representation is written
\lambda=[]
. A
Q
-dimensional vector whose components are permuted by
S_{Q}
belongs to the representation
[1]+[]
. More generally, a representation
\lambda
can be interpreted in terms of tensors with
|\lambda|
indices, each of which takes
Q
values
[
23
]
.

The tensor product of two representations of
S_{Q}
for generic
Q\in\mathbb{C}
can be written as

\displaystyle\lambda\otimes\mu=\sum_{\nu}M_{\lambda,\mu,\nu}\nu\ ,

(3.2)

where the reduced Kronecker coefficients
M_{\lambda,\mu,\nu}\in\mathbb{N}
are
Q
-independent, and invariant under permutations of the three indices
\lambda,\mu,\nu
. For example, the simplest non-trivial tensor product is

\displaystyle[1]\otimes[1]=[2]+[1^{2}]+[1]+[]\ .

(3.3)

Reduced Kronecker coefficients can be computed by at least two (not very easy) methods
[
24
,
25
]
. Fortunately, they are implemented in the computer algebra system SageMath
[
26
]
.

For
Q\in\mathbb{N}
, the tensor products of
S_{Q}
representations involve
Q
-dependent coefficients called Kronecker coefficients, which tend toward reduced Kronecker coefficients as
Q
becomes large. Therefore, the limit
Q\to\infty
gives us access to generic values of
Q
from integer values.

Characters as symmetric polynomials

By definition, characters of the symmetric group are functions of the finitely many elements of that group. However, it is possible to define symmetric polynomials of arbitrarily many variables, which encode the characters of
S_{Q}
for large enough
Q\in\mathbb{N}

[
27
]
. Calling these polynomials
\chi_{\lambda}^{S_{Q}}
(rather than
\tilde{s}_{\lambda}
in
[
27
]
), this means that for any
Q\geq|\lambda|+\lambda_{1}
and for any
g\in S_{Q}
with eigenvalues
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{Q}
, we have

\displaystyle\chi_{\lambda}^{S_{Q}}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{Q})=\operatorname{Tr}_{\lambda}(g)\ .

(3.4)

We view the symmetric polynomials
\chi_{\lambda}^{S_{Q}}
as the characters of the symmetric group for generic
Q\in\mathbb{C}
. They are
Q
-independent, and they form a ring whose structure constants are the reduced Kronecker coefficients (
3.2
), i.e.
\chi^{S_{Q}}_{\lambda}\chi^{S_{Q}}_{\mu}=\sum_{\nu}M_{\lambda,\mu,\nu}\chi^{S_{Q}}_{\nu}
.

The characters
\chi_{\lambda}^{S_{Q}}
are a basis of the space of symmetric polynomials, therefore they are linearly related to the Schur polynomials. In this basis, the decomposition of a symmetric polynomial
p_{r}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{r}
is
[
27
]
(Theorem 21)

\displaystyle p_{r}=\sum_{d|r}\left(1+\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}(-1)^{k}\chi^{S_{Q}}_{[d-k,1^{k}]}\right)\ ,

(3.5)

where the first sum is over the integer divisors of
r
, including
d=1
and
d=r
. This motivates us to define the following formal representations of
S_{Q}
,

\displaystyle\boxed{\xi_{d}=[]+\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}(-1)^{k}[d-k,1^{k}]}\ ,

(3.6)

such that
\sum_{d|r}\chi_{\xi_{d}}^{S_{Q}}=p_{r}
. (Conversely, it is possible to express
\chi_{\xi_{d}}^{S_{Q}}
as a linear combination of
p_{r}
using the Möbius inversion formula.)
For large enough values of
Q\in\mathbb{N}
, this implies

\displaystyle\boxed{\sum_{d|r}\operatorname{Tr}_{\xi_{d}}(g)=\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]+[]}(g^{r})}\ .

(3.7)

In the particular case
g=\text{id}
, this reduces to

\displaystyle\dim^{S_{Q}}(\xi_{d})=Q\delta_{d,1}\ .

(3.8)

The first few examples of the representations
\xi_{d}
are

\displaystyle\xi_{1}=[]+[1]\quad,\quad\xi_{2}=[]+[2]-[1^{2}]\quad,\quad\xi_{3}=[]+[3]-[21]+[1^{3}]\ .

(3.9)

These combinations of irreducible representations are formal, since the coefficients may be negative, although they are integer.

3.2
Action of the symmetric group on the space of states

The symmetries of the Potts CFT include the symmetric group
S_{Q}
, and the conformal algebra
\mathfrak{C}_{c}
, which is a product of the left- and right-moving Virasoro algebras. The CFT’s space of states is therefore a representation of
S_{Q}\times\mathfrak{C}_{c}
, which we want to decompose into indecomposable representations.

General structure of the space of states

Together with the existence of degenerate fields, the torus partition function is enough for determining the representations of the conformal algebra that appear in the space of states
[
11
]
. These representations belong to the same families
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,s\rangle},\mathcal{W}_{(r,s)}
(
2.2
) that we introduced for the
O(n)
CFT.
The space of states of the Potts CFT can be written in terms of these representations, together with representations of
S_{Q}
:

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{S}^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}\underset{\mathfrak{C}_{c}\times S_{Q}}{=}\bigoplus_{s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,s\rangle}\otimes[]\oplus\bigoplus_{s\in\mathbb{N}+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{W}_{(0,s)}\otimes[1]\oplus\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbb{N}+2}\bigoplus_{s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{W}_{(r,s)}\otimes\Xi_{(r,s)}}\ .

(3.10)

Here the representations of
S_{Q}
are the trivial representation
[]
, the standard representation
[1]
, and a family of representations
\Xi_{(r,s)}
: linear combinations of irreducible finite-dimensional representations, with positive integer coefficients that do not depend on
Q

[
20
,
21
]
.

The symmetry of the model under exchanging the left- and right-moving Virasoro algebras leads to the relation

\displaystyle\Xi_{(r,s)}=\Xi_{(r,-s)}\ .

(3.11)

Further constraints come from the existence of a degenerate field
V_{\langle 1,2\rangle}
that transforms in the degenerate representation
\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,2\rangle}
of the conformal algebra. The fusion of such a field with a non-diagonal primary field
V^{N}_{(r,s)}\in\mathcal{W}_{(r,s)}
yields
[
22
]

\displaystyle V_{\langle 1,2\rangle}\times V^{N}_{(r,s)}\simeq V^{N}_{(r,s-1)}+V^{N}_{(r,s+1)}\ .

(3.12)

In the Potts CFT,
V_{\langle 1,2\rangle}
is an
S_{Q}
singlet. This implies that
V^{N}_{(r,s\pm 1)}
transform in the same way as
V^{N}_{(r,s)}
under
S_{Q}
, therefore

\displaystyle\Xi_{(r,s)}=\Xi_{(r,s+1)}\ .

(3.13)

This suggests that we combine the representations of the conformal algebra into the larger representations

\displaystyle{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}$}}}}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}=\bigoplus_{s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\mathcal{R}_{\langle 1,s\rangle}\quad,\quad{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}}_{(0,\frac{1}{2})}=\bigoplus_{s\in\mathbb{N}+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{W}_{(0,s)}\quad,\quad{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}}_{(r,s)}\underset{r\neq 0}{=}\bigoplus_{s^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}+s}\mathcal{W}_{(r,s)}\ .

(3.14)

These can be interpreted as indecomposable representations of the interchiral algebra
{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}$}}}}_{\beta^{2}}
, which is obtained from the conformal algebra by adding the degenerate field
V_{\langle 1,2\rangle}

[
10
]
. The space of states can then be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}\underset{{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}$}}}}_{\beta^{2}}\times S_{Q}}{=}{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}$}}}}_{\langle 1,1\rangle}\otimes[]\oplus{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}}_{(0,\frac{1}{2})}\otimes[1]\oplus\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbb{N}+2}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\\
-\frac{1}{2}<s\leq\frac{1}{2}\end{subarray}}{\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}}_{(r,s)}\otimes\Xi_{(r,s)}\ .

(3.15)

Action of the symmetric group

The action of the orthogonal group on the space of states is encoded in the family of representations
\Xi_{(r,s)}
. Just as in the case of the
O(n)
CFT, it is possible to guess these representations from the known torus partition function of
[
3
]
: the idea is to replace each occurrence of the parameter
Q
with a well-chosen
Q
-dimensional representation of the form
\sum_{d|r}\xi_{d}
. We will not do this in detail, because we are about to derive the result from the twisted partition function. For the moment, we only display the result:

\displaystyle\boxed{\Xi_{(r,s)}=(-1)^{r}\delta_{s\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{r+1}{2}}[1]+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r-1}e^{2\pi ir^{\prime}s}U_{\mathrm{gcd}(r,r^{\prime})}\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{d|\frac{r}{\mathrm{gcd}(r,r^{\prime})}}\xi_{d}-2\right)}\ ,\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}r\in\mathbb{N}+2\ ,\\
s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\ ,\end{array}\right.

where
\xi_{d}
(
3.6
) is a formal representation of
S_{Q}
, and
U_{d}
(
2.26
) is a Chebyshev polynomial. Alternatively, using Ramanujan’s sums (
2.32
), this can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Xi_{(r,s)}=(-1)^{r}\delta_{s\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{r+1}{2}}[1]+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g,g^{\prime}|r\\
gg^{\prime}=r\end{subarray}}\varphi_{rs}(g)U_{g^{\prime}}\left(\textstyle{\sum}_{d|g}\xi_{d}-2\right)\ .

(3.18)

Since
\varphi_{rs}(g)\in\mathbb{Z}
, and the Chebyshev polynomials have integer coefficients,
\Xi_{(r,s)}
is a combination of irreducible, finite-dimensional representations with coefficients in
\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}
. The coefficients should actually be positive integers: in examples, we can see that this is actually the case.

Explicit examples

Given the relations (
3.11
) and (
3.13
), the cases
0\leq s\leq\frac{1}{2}
already sample all different representations
\Xi_{(r,s)}
. The number of different cases is further restricted by the fact that
\Xi_{(r,s)}
only depends on
s
through
\mathrm{gcd}(r,rs)
.
We now write these reprentations according to Eq. (
3.2
). Here we display the results for
r\leq 6
. The computer code
[
16
]
can easily reach at least
r\simeq 10
.

\displaystyle\Xi_{(2,0)}

\displaystyle=[2]\ ,

(3.19a)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(2,\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=[1^{2}]\ ,

(3.19b)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(3,0)}

\displaystyle=[3]+[1^{3}]\ ,

(3.19c)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(3,\frac{1}{3})}

\displaystyle=[21]\ ,

(3.19d)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(4,0)}

\displaystyle=[4]+[2^{2}]+[21^{2}]+[3]+[21]+2[2]+[1]+[]\ ,

(3.19e)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(4,\frac{1}{4})}

\displaystyle=[31]+[21^{2}]+[21]+[1^{3}]+[1^{2}]\ ,

(3.19f)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(4,\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=[31]+[2^{2}]+[1^{4}]+[3]+[21]+[2]+[1^{2}]+[1]\ ,

(3.19g)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(5,0)}

\displaystyle=[5]+[32]+2[31^{2}]+[2^{2}1]+[1^{5}]+[4]+3[31]+2[2^{2}]+3[21^{2}]+[1^{4}]

\displaystyle\quad+2[3]+4[21]+2[1^{3}]+2[2]+2[1^{2}]+[1]\ ,

(3.19h)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(5,\frac{1}{5})}

\displaystyle=[41]+[32]+[31^{2}]+[2^{2}1]+[21^{3}]+[4]+3[31]+2[2^{2}]+3[21^{2}]+[1^{4}]

\displaystyle\quad+2[3]+4[21]+2[1^{3}]+2[2]+2[1^{2}]+[1]\ ,

(3.19i)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(6,0)}

\displaystyle=[6]+2[42]+2[41^{2}]+[3^{2}]+2[321]+2[31^{3}]+2[2^{3}]+[2^{2}1^{2}]+[21^{4}]

\displaystyle\quad+2[5]+6[41]+8[32]+8[31^{2}]+8[2^{2}1]+6[21^{3}]+2[1^{5}]+7[4]+14[31]+11[2^{2}]

\displaystyle\quad+13[21^{2}]+6[1^{4}]+11[3]+15[21]+8[1^{3}]+10[2]+6[1^{2}]+5[1]+3[]\ ,

(3.19j)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(6,\frac{1}{6})}

\displaystyle=[51]+[42]+2[41^{2}]+[3^{2}]+3[321]+[31^{3}]+2[2^{2}1^{2}]+[21^{4}]

\displaystyle\quad+[5]+6[41]+7[32]+10[31^{2}]+7[2^{2}1]+6[21^{3}]+[1^{5}]+3[4]+15[31]+8[2^{2}]

\displaystyle\quad+16[21^{2}]+4[1^{4}]+6[3]+16[21]+9[1^{3}]+5[2]+8[1^{2}]+2[1]\ ,

(3.19k)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(6,\frac{1}{3})}

\displaystyle=[51]+2[42]+[41^{2}]+3[321]+2[31^{3}]+[2^{3}]+[2^{2}1^{2}]+[21^{4}]

\displaystyle\quad+2[5]+6[41]+8[32]+8[31^{2}]+8[2^{2}1]+6[21^{3}]+2[1^{5}]+6[4]+14[31]+12[2^{2}]

\displaystyle\quad+13[21^{2}]+5[1^{4}]+9[3]+17[21]+6[1^{3}]+9[2]+6[1^{2}]+4[1]+[]\ ,

(3.19l)

\displaystyle\Xi_{(6,\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=[51]+[42]+2[41^{2}]+2[3^{2}]+2[321]+2[31^{3}]+[2^{3}]+2[2^{2}1^{2}]+[1^{6}]

\displaystyle\quad+[5]+6[41]+7[32]+10[31^{2}]+7[2^{2}1]+6[21^{3}]+[1^{5}]+4[4]+15[31]+7[2^{2}]

\displaystyle\quad+16[21^{2}]+5[1^{4}]+7[3]+15[21]+10[1^{3}]+5[2]+9[1^{2}]+3[1]\ .

(3.19m)

3.3
Twisted partition function of the Potts CFT

Definition and character decomposition

Let us define the twisted partition function of the Potts CFT on a torus of modulus
\tau
as

\displaystyle Z^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathcal{S}^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}}\left(e^{2\pi i\tau(L_{0}-\frac{c}{24})}e^{2\pi i\bar{\tau}(\bar{L}_{0}-\frac{c}{24})}\cdot g\right)\ ,

(3.20)

where
g\in S_{Q}
, and
L_{0},\bar{L}_{0}
are elements of the conformal algebra
\mathfrak{C}_{c}
. The decomposition (
3.10
) of the space of states
\mathcal{S}^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}
into representations of
S_{Q}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{c}
leads to a decomposition of the partition function into characters,

\displaystyle Z^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\chi_{\langle 1,s\rangle}(\tau)+\sum_{s\in\mathbb{N}+\frac{1}{2}}\chi^{N}_{(0,s)}(\tau)\chi^{S_{Q}}_{[1]}(g)+\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}+2}\sum_{s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}}\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)\chi^{S_{Q}}_{\Xi_{(r,s)}}(g)\ ,

(3.21)

where the characters
\chi_{\langle 1,s\rangle}(\tau),\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)
of the conformal algebra were defined in Eq. (
2.39
).

The modular invariant partition function in
[
3
]
is the special case
Z^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,\text{id})
. In its decomposition into characters, the
S_{Q}
characters reduce to

\displaystyle\chi^{S_{Q}}_{\Xi_{(r,s)}}(\text{id})=\dim^{S_{Q}}\Xi_{(r,s)}=(-1)^{r}\delta_{s\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{r+1}{2}}(Q-1)+\frac{1}{r}\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r-1}e^{2\pi ir^{\prime}s}U_{\mathrm{gcd}(r,r^{\prime})}\left(Q-2\right)\ .

(3.22)

These dimensions are polynomial functions of
Q
.
For example

\displaystyle\dim^{S_{Q}}\Xi_{(2,0)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}Q(Q-3)\ ,

(3.23a)

\displaystyle\dim^{S_{Q}}\Xi_{(2,\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}(Q-1)(Q-4)\ ,

(3.23b)

\displaystyle\dim^{S_{Q}}\Xi_{(3,0)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{3}(Q-1)(Q-2)(Q-3)\ ,

(3.23c)

\displaystyle\dim^{S_{Q}}\Xi_{(3,\frac{1}{3})}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{3}Q(Q-2)(Q-4)\ ,

(3.23d)

\displaystyle\dim^{S_{Q}}\Xi_{(4,0)}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4}Q(Q-2)(Q-3)^{2}\ ,

(3.23e)

\displaystyle\dim^{S_{Q}}\Xi_{(4,\frac{1}{4})}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4}Q(Q-1)(Q-3)(Q-4)\,,

(3.23f)

\displaystyle\dim^{S_{Q}}\Xi_{(4,\frac{1}{2})}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4}(Q-1)^{2}(Q-2)(Q-4)\ ,

(3.23g)

in agreement with (
3.19
). However, knowing these dimensions is not enough for determining the corresponding representations.

On the other hand, the character
\chi^{S_{Q}}_{\Xi_{(r,s)}}(g)
fully determines the representation
\Xi_{(r,s)}
. In order to prove the conjecture, it is therefore sufficient to compute the twisted partition function
Z^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)
and check that it agrees with (
3.21
). Due to the inclusion of a group element
g
, the proof will only be valid for
Q\in\mathbb{N}
. However, just like reduced Kronecker coefficients, the structure of
\Xi_{(r,s)}
is
Q
-independent for
Q
generic, and can be accessed from integer values of
Q
by taking the limit
Q\to\infty
.

Loop representation of the Potts model

For
Q
in a rather large region of the complex plane, including for
Q\in(0,4)
, the Potts CFT is the infinite-size limit of the
Q
-state Potts model at the critical coupling constant,
J=J_{c}

[
7
]
. The simplest version of the lattice model is defined on the square lattice, in which case
[
1
]

\displaystyle e^{J_{c}}=1\pm\sqrt{Q}\ .

(3.24)

The Potts model’s loop representation is very similar to that of the
O(n)
model. This allows us to compute the Potts CFT’s partition function as a modification of the
O(n)
CFT’s partition function
[
3
]
. We will now review this computation, while generalizing it to the twisted partition function.

In its original formulation, the Potts model describes variables
s_{i}\in\{1,2,\dots,Q\}
(called spins) on a square lattice, with an interaction energy
-J\delta_{s_{i},s_{j}}
between neighbouring sites. In order to compute the twisted partition function on the torus
\mathbb{T}=\frac{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}}
, we single out a topologically non-trivial oriented cycle on the torus, for example a cycle whose class in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is
(0,1)
. For a given
g\in S_{Q}
,
we then modify the interaction energy to
-J\delta_{s_{i},g(s_{j})}
whenever the edge
(ij)
crosses the chosen cycle. We will now follow this modification when reformulating the Potts model as a random cluster model, and then as a loop model.

The random cluster formulation of the Potts model is obtained by writing the edge Boltzmann weight as

\displaystyle e^{J\delta_{s_{i},s_{j}}}=1+(e^{J}-1)\delta_{s_{i},s_{j}}

(3.25)

and replacing the sum over spins
\{s_{i}\}
with a sum over
A
, where
A\subseteq E
denotes the subset of edges for which the term
(e^{J}-1)\delta_{s_{i},s_{j}}
is taken in the expansion of
\prod_{(ij)\in E}e^{J\delta_{s_{i},s_{j}}}
. For any lattice, this leads to
[
28
]

\displaystyle Z^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}=\sum_{A\subseteq E}(e^{J}-1)^{|A|}Q^{k(A)}\ ,

(3.26)

where
k(A)
denotes the total number of clusters in
A
.

Now on the torus, let us consider a topologically non-trivial cluster whose class in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T})
is
(m,m^{\prime})
. (See Figure (
2.45
).) Let us start at a site
i_{0}
of spin
s_{i_{0}}
, and go once around the cluster. Along the way,
the cycle where spins jump by the permutation
g
is crossed
m
times, and we come back to our starting point with the spin
g^{m}(s_{i_{0}})=s_{i_{0}}
. The number of possible configurations for this cluster is therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{Q}\delta_{s,g^{m}(s)}=\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]+[]}g^{m}\ ,

(3.27)

and this is the cluster weight in the twisted partition function.

The loop formulation of the Potts model is obtained by drawing the non-intersecting loops that separate clusters from dual clusters. In the following example the original square lattice is in black, the edge subset
A\subseteq E
is in red, and the dual clusters in green live on the dual lattice. The loops in blue live on the corresponding medial graph, with each loop separating a cluster from a dual cluster:

(3.28)

On the torus, let us assume that there exists a cluster that includes a cycle of class
(m,m^{\prime})\in\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T})
, and no other non-trivial cycle. Then all topologically non-trivial loops and clusters are of that class, with twice as many such loops as clusters. This is no longer the case for a configuration where a cluster contains at least two topologically independent cycles: this is called a cluster with cross topology. It is not hard to show that the first fundamental group of such a cluster includes the full
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T})=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and that the weight of the cluster is
\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]+[]}g
. All loops are topologically trivial if and only if there exists a cluster or a dual cluster with cross topology. Representing the torus as a parallelogram, let us draw a cluster with cross topology:

(3.29)

Twisted partition function

For a configuration
\mathcal{C}
, we write

•

S_{0}
the number of clusters whose images in
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T})
are trivial,

•

S
the number of clusters whose images are non-trivial, excluding clusters with cross topology,

•

(m,m^{\prime})
the corresponding element of
\pi_{1}(\mathbb{T})
,

•

|A|
the number of edges in the subset
A\subseteq E
.

Taking the critical value for the coupling, the twisted partition function of the
Q
-state Potts model on the torus
\mathbb{T}=\frac{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}}
with
\Sigma
lattice sites is

\displaystyle Z_{\text{lattice}}^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)=Q^{-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma}\sum_{\mathcal{C}}Q^{\frac{1}{2}|A|(\mathcal{C})}Q^{S_{0}(\mathcal{C})}\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]+[]}g^{m(\mathcal{C})}\right)^{S(\mathcal{C})}\left\{1+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{S(\mathcal{C}),0}\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g\right\}\ .

(3.30)

In the last factor, the term
\frac{1}{2}\delta_{S(\mathcal{C}),0}\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g
accounts for the cluster with cross topology. If such a cluster exists, then no other topologically non-trivial cluster can exist, therefore
S=0
. Conversely, if
S=0
, we have two mutually exclusive cases: there exists either a dual cluster with cross topology, or a cluster with cross topology. If
S=0
, the last factor can be rewritten as
\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]+[]}g\right)
, with each term corresponding to one of the two cases.

Let us rewrite the twisted partition function in terms of the numbers
N_{0},N
of loops, topologically trivial and non-trivial respectively. We obviously have
N=2S
, and using the Euler characteristic we can show
N_{0}=2S_{0}+|A|-\Sigma
, which leads to

\displaystyle Z_{\text{lattice}}^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)=\sum_{\mathcal{C}|N(\mathcal{C})\in 2\mathbb{N}}\sqrt{Q}^{N_{0}(\mathcal{C})}\left\{\sqrt{\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]+[]}g^{m(\mathcal{C})}}^{N(\mathcal{C})}+\frac{1}{2}\delta_{N(\mathcal{C}),0}\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g\right\}\ .

(3.31)

Compared to the twisted partition function of the
O(n)
model (
2.46
), the main differences are the restriction to even numbers of topologically non-trivial loops, and the term with
\delta_{N(\mathcal{C}),0}
.

Let us relate this lattice partition function to the loop sums
Z^{M,M^{\prime}}_{\text{lattice}}(\tau,n)
(
2.47
), where we assume that the coupling
K
takes its critical value. We split our loops into two possible orientations
N=N_{+}+N_{-}
with
N_{+}\equiv N_{-}\bmod 2
, and use Eq. (
2.48
) for the two weights that appear in the form (
3.31
) of the partition function:
z=\sqrt{\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]+[]}g^{m(\mathcal{C})}}
in the first term, and
z=0
in the second term, which is proportional to
\delta_{N(\mathcal{C}),0}=0^{N(\mathcal{C})}
. Using Eq. (
2.28
) for
U_{d}(0)
and
writing
(M,M^{\prime})=(m(N_{+}-N_{-}),m^{\prime}(N_{+}-N_{-}))
, we obtain

Z_{\text{lattice}}^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{M,M^{\prime}\in 2\mathbb{Z}}Z^{M,M^{\prime}}_{\text{lattice}}\left(\tau,\sqrt{Q}\right)\Bigg\{U_{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})}\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]+[]}g^{\frac{M}{\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime})}}-2\right)\\
+\cos\tfrac{\pi}{2}(\mathrm{gcd}(M,M^{\prime}))\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g\Bigg\}\ .

(3.32)

Critical limit

For the sake of simplicity, we have already taken the coupling to be critical, and we further send the lattice size to infinity.
This allows us to use the expression (
2.50
) for the critical limit of
Z^{M,M^{\prime}}_{\text{lattice}}\left(\tau,\sqrt{Q}\right)
. We then perform a Poisson resummation over
M^{\prime}
if
M=0
, or over
k
such that
M^{\prime}=kM+2r^{\prime}
with
r^{\prime}\in\{0,1,\dots,\frac{M}{2}-1\}
if
M\neq 0
. Introducing the integer
r
such that
M=2r
, and separately considering the two terms in Eq. (
3.32
), we obtain four terms:

\displaystyle\left.Z^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)\right|^{\text{Term $1$}}_{M=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}\left|\frac{e^{2\pi i\tau P_{(1,s)}^{2}}}{\eta(\tau)}\right|^{2}=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\chi_{\langle 1,s\rangle}(\tau)+\sum_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi_{(1,s)}^{N}(\tau)\ ,

(3.33)

\displaystyle\left.Z^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)\right|^{\text{Term $1$}}_{M\neq 0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}\sum_{s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}}\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r-1}e^{2\pi ir^{\prime}s}U_{\mathrm{gcd}(r,r^{\prime})}\left(\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]+[]}g^{\frac{r}{\mathrm{gcd}(r,r^{\prime})}}-2\right)\ ,

(3.34)

\displaystyle\left.Z^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)\right|^{\text{Term $2$}}_{M=0}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\chi_{(0,s)}^{N}(\tau)\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g\ ,

(3.35)

\displaystyle\left.Z^{\text{$Q$-Potts}}(\tau,g)\right|^{\text{Term $2$}}_{M\neq 0}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{r\in 2\mathbb{N}^{*}}\sum_{s\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)-\sum_{r\in 2\mathbb{N}+1}\sum_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}\chi^{N}_{(r,s)}(\tau)\right)\operatorname{Tr}_{[1]}g\ .

(3.36)

Summing the four terms, we recover the twisted partition function (
3.21
). In particular, the terms that involve
\chi^{N}_{(1,s)}(\tau)
all cancel.

4
Algebraic approach to the
O(n)
model

There are a number of different constructions of the
O(n)
model on the lattice, which lead to the same critical limit
[
2
,
29
,
4
]
. For simplicity, we consider the dense
O(n)
universality class, which is contained in all these lattice models.
The space of states is

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}_{L}=[1]^{\otimes L}\ .

(4.1)

This space describes the states of a chain of
L
spins, where each spin lives in the vector representation
[1]
. (For the dilute model with spins in
[1]\oplus[]
, see the conclusion.)
The corresponding model may be called the dense unoriented loop model
[
4
]
. This model admits either periodic or non-periodic boundary conditions, and we refer to these cases as the closed and open spin chain, respectively. While our main interest is in the closed chain, we will also deal with the open chain, which has simpler algebraic properties.

4.1
Action of diagram algebras on the space of states

We now introduce the diagram algebras that act on the space of states of the
O(n)
model. We will focus on the algebraic properties of these algebras and their representations. For readers not already familiar with diagram algebras, we now recommend reading Appendix
A
, which reviews diagram algebras and the eponymous diagrams.

The relevant diagram algebras

The algebra of linear maps on
\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}_{L}
that commute with
O(n)
is the Brauer algebra

\displaystyle O(n)^{*}=\mathcal{B}_{L}(n)\ .

(4.2)

The Brauer algebra is generated by
2L-2
elements
p_{i},e_{i}
with
i=1,\dots,L-1
. The generator
p_{i}
permutes the factors
i
and
i+1
in
[1]^{\otimes L}
. For
n
integer, the generator
e_{i}
acts as follows on the factors
i
and
i+1
of
[1]^{\otimes L}
, while leaving the other factors invariant:

\displaystyle e_{i}:\quad\begin{array}[]{lll}[1]_{i}\otimes[1]_{i+1}&\to&[1]_{i}\otimes[1]_{i+1}\\
w_{k}\otimes w_{\ell}&\mapsto&\delta_{k\ell}\sum_{m=1}^{n}w_{m}\otimes w_{m}\end{array}\ ,

where
(w_{k})_{k}
is an orthonormal basis of
[1]
. As a diagram algebra, the Brauer algebra exists for any
n\in\mathbb{C}
, and the generators
p_{i},e_{i}
are described by the diagrams (
A.3
), (
A.4
).

The Brauer algebra includes the permutation group
S_{L}
, and ignores the geometry of the problem. If we assume that the
L
spins are arrayed in a chain, we can no longer permute them. The symmetries of the system must then be described by a smaller algebra, which also takes into account the boundary conditions. If we have an open chain with free boundary conditions, the relevant algebra is the Temperley–Lieb algebra
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
, which has
L-1
generators
e_{i}
. For a closed chain with periodic boundary conditions, the relevant algebra is the unoriented Jones–Temperley–Lieb algebra
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
, which is obtained from
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
by adding two generators: a generator
e_{L}
(
A.15
) which contracts the sites
L,1
in the same manner that
e_{i}
contracts
i,i+1
, and a generator
u
(
A.16
) which cyclically translates all the sites by one unit, and obeys
u^{L}=1
.

The diagrammatic representations of the algebras
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
and
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
directly correspond to the representation of the
O(n)
model as a loop model in the transfer matrix approach: the absence of the permutation generators means that loops do not cross, and the relations
e_{i}^{2}=ne_{i}
means that loops have weight
n
. However, in contrast to our construction of loops in Figure (
3.28
), the representation
[1]
now lives on the loops themselves, rather than on sites between the loops. This difference does not affect the critical limit.

Therefore, we expect that the algebras
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
and
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
describe symmetries of the
O(n)
model. In particular, in the critical limit, we expect

\displaystyle\lim_{\text{critical}}u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)=\widetilde{\mathfrak{C}}_{\beta^{2}}\ ,

(4.5)

where the parameter
\beta^{2}
of the interchiral algebra is related to
n
by Eq. (
1.4
).

Representations of the Brauer algebra

By Schur–Weyl duality, the space of states (
4.1
) decomposes into irreducible representations of
\mathcal{B}_{L}(n)\times O(n)
as
[
30
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}_{L}\underset{\mathcal{B}_{L}(n)\times O(n)}{=}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\lambda|\leq L\\
|\lambda|\equiv L\bmod 2\end{subarray}}B^{(L)}_{\lambda}\otimes\lambda\ ,

(4.6)

where the sum is over partitions
\lambda
, which we identify with irreducible representations of
O(n)
with generic
n\in\mathbb{C}
. The irreducible finite-dimensional representations
B^{(L)}_{\lambda}
of the Brauer algebra are also labelled by partitions: we will say more on their structure when it comes to computing branching rules in Section
4.2
. For the moment, let us point out that their dimensions appear as coefficients in the decomposition of
\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}_{L}=[1]^{\otimes L}
into irreducible representations of
O(n)
,

\displaystyle[1]^{\otimes L}\underset{O(n)}{=}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\lambda|\leq L\\
|\lambda|\equiv L\bmod 2\end{subarray}}\left(\dim B^{(L)}_{\lambda}\right)\lambda\ .

(4.7)

For example,
[1]^{\otimes 3}=[3]+2[21]+[111]+3[1]
tells us that
\dim B^{(3)}_{[21]}=2
.

Representations of the Temperley–Lieb algebra

We now want to decompose the representations of the Brauer algebra into irreducible representations of the smaller
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
and
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
algebras. In the case of the Temperley–Lieb algebra
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
, the branching rules are explicitly known
[
31
]
. The irreducible representations of
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
are called standard modules, and parametrized by positive integers in
L-2\mathbb{N}
: for ease of comparison with the CFT, we will use half-integers
r\in\frac{L}{2}-\mathbb{N}
instead, and write
W^{(L)}_{r}
for the standard module of dimension (
A.10
).
The decomposition takes the form

\displaystyle\boxed{B^{(L)}_{\lambda}\underset{\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)}{=}f_{\lambda}\bigoplus^{\frac{L}{2}}_{r=\frac{|\lambda|}{2}}c^{|\lambda|}_{r}W^{(L)}_{r}}\ ,

(4.8)

where the sum runs by increments of
1
. Here,
f_{\lambda}
is the number of standard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda
, given by the hook length formula

\displaystyle f_{\lambda}=\frac{|\lambda|!}{\prod_{b\in\lambda}\text{hook length}(b)}\ ,

(4.9)

where the product is over the boxes in the Young diagram. Moreover, the coefficents
c^{\ell}_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
are

\displaystyle c^{\ell}_{r}=\sum_{r^{\prime}=0}^{r-\frac{\ell}{2}}(-1)^{r^{\prime}}\binom{2r-r^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}}\binom{2r-2r^{\prime}}{\ell}(2r-2r^{\prime}-\ell-1)!!\ .

(4.10)

It goes without saying that these integer coefficients do not depend on the complex number
n
. More remarkably, they also do not depend on the lattice size
L
: this makes it particularly easy to take the critical limit.

Inserting our decomposition of
B^{(L)}_{\lambda}
into the decomposition (
4.6
) of the space of states, we obtain

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}_{L}\underset{\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)\times O(n)}{=}\bigoplus_{r=\left\{\frac{L}{2}\right\}}^{\frac{L}{2}}W^{(L)}_{r}\otimes\Lambda_{r}}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\boxed{\Lambda_{r}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\lambda|\leq 2r\\
|\lambda|\equiv 2r\bmod 2\end{subarray}}f_{\lambda}c^{|\lambda|}_{r}\lambda}\ ,

(4.11)

where the sum over
r
runs by increments of
1
, starting from the fractional part of
\frac{L}{2}
.
This implies
\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}_{L}\underset{O(n)}{=}\bigoplus_{r=\left\{\frac{L}{2}\right\}}^{\frac{L}{2}}\left(\dim W^{(L)}_{r}\right)\Lambda_{r}
, from which
\Lambda_{r}
may be recursively deduced, assuming we know the dimensions (
A.10
) of the Temperley–Lieb representations.
The first few representations
\Lambda_{r}
are

\displaystyle\Lambda_{0}

\displaystyle=[]\ ,

(4.12a)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=[1]\ ,

(4.12b)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{1}

\displaystyle=[2]+[1^{2}]\ ,

(4.12c)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\frac{3}{2}}

\displaystyle=[3]+2[21]+[1^{3}]+[1]\ ,

(4.12d)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{2}

\displaystyle=[4]+3[31]+2[2^{2}]+3[21^{2}]+[1^{4}]+3[2]+3[1^{2}]+[]\ .

(4.12e)

They obey the simple property
[
31
]
(Proposition 3.2)

\displaystyle[1]\otimes\Lambda_{r}=\Lambda_{r-\frac{1}{2}}\oplus\Lambda_{r+\frac{1}{2}}\ .

(4.13)

Using the associativity of the tensor product, this implies

\displaystyle\Lambda_{r_{1}}\otimes\Lambda_{r_{2}}=\bigoplus_{r=|r_{1}-r_{2}|}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}\Lambda_{r}\ .

(4.14)

As the decomposition (
4.11
) of the space of states suggests,
\Lambda_{r}
can be interpreted as an irreducible representation of the commutant
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}^{*}(n)
of the Temperley–Lieb algebra
[
4
]
. The closure of the representations
\Lambda_{r}
under tensor products is a consequence of the existence of a Hopf algebra structure on
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}^{*}(n)
, i.e. of a coassociative comultiplication. The comultiplication is constructed by joining two open chains by their end points: this will no longer be possible with closed chains
[
32
]
.

Representations of the unoriented Jones–Temperley–Lieb algebra

The irreducible representations
W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}
of
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
are parametrized by positive half-integers
r\in\frac{L}{2}-\mathbb{N}
, and by a rational number
s
called the pseudo-momentum. The possible values of the pseudo-momentum depends on
r
: we have
s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\cap(-1,1]
if
r\neq 0
, and by convention we set
s=0
if
r=0
. The decomposition of an irreducible representation of the Brauer algebra reads

\displaystyle\boxed{B^{(L)}_{\lambda}\underset{u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)}{=}\bigoplus_{r=\frac{|\lambda|}{2}}^{\frac{L}{2}}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\\
-1<s\leq 1\end{subarray}}c^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}}\ .

(4.15)

The coefficients
c^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}
are positive integer numbers, which do not depend on
n
. We moreover expect that they do not depend on
L
, as was the case in the decomposition of
B^{(L)}_{\lambda}
into representations of the Temperley–Lieb algebra.
We will give two methods for computing these coefficients: via a closed combinatorial formula in Section
4.2
, and via transfer matrix techniques in Appendix
B.1
. For the moment, let us proceed with decomposing the space of states, assuming
c^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}
to be given.
Inserting the decomposition of Brauer representations into Eq. (
4.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{S}^{O(n)}_{L}\underset{u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)\times O(n)}{=}\bigoplus_{r=\left\{\frac{L}{2}\right\}}^{\frac{L}{2}}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\\
-1<s\leq 1\end{subarray}}W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}\otimes\Lambda_{(r,s)}}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\boxed{\Lambda_{(r,s)}=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}|\lambda|\leq 2r\\
|\lambda|\equiv 2r\bmod 2\end{subarray}}c^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}\lambda}\ .

(4.16)

The representations
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
of
O(n)
that we just defined should coincide with the representations
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
(
2.2
) that appeared in the spectrum (
2.20
) of the
O(n)
CFT. Moreover, the large
L
limit of the representation
W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}
should coincide with a representation of the interchiral algebra,

\displaystyle\lim_{\text{critical}}W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}=\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{(r,s)}\ .

(4.17)

One may doubt the plausibility of this relation, on the grounds that
W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}
is always irreducible, while
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}_{(r,s)}
is logarithmic and therefore reducible for
(r,s)\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. However, logarithmic features can emerge at
L=\infty
while being absent for any finite
L
. A necessary requirement for this is that two eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian differ for finite
L
, while having the same limit
[
12
]
.

We can obtain some nontrivial information on
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
by comparing our decomposition (
4.16
) of the space of states with the decomposition (
4.11
) into representations of the Temperley–Lieb algebra. As reviewed in Eq. (
A.29
), the representation
W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}
of
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
is constructed by gluing the representations
W^{(L)}_{r},W^{(L)}_{r+1},\dots,W^{(L)}_{\frac{L}{2}}
of
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
. The pseudo-momentum
s
labels different ways of gluing these representations. (For the special case of
W_{(0,0)}^{L}
, see Eq. (
A.30
).)
We will review the general definition of the pseudo-momentum in Appendix
A
. Here we only need to know that
in the case
r=\frac{L}{2}
, the pseudo-momentum corresponds to an eigenvalue of the translation generator
u
(
A.16
) of
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
,

\displaystyle\left(u-e^{\pi is}\right)W^{(L)}_{(\frac{L}{2},s)}=0\ .

(4.18)

Inserting Eq.
A.29
into the decomposition (
4.16
), and comparing with the decomposition (
4.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle\boxed{\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\\
-1<s\leq 1\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{(r,s)}=\Lambda_{r}-\delta_{r\geq\frac{3}{2}}\Lambda_{r-1}}\ .

(4.19)

Using the recursion relation (
4.13
) for
\Lambda_{r}
, we deduce the identity

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\\
-1<s\leq 1\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{(r,s)}=\left(\delta_{r,1}-\delta_{r,0}\right)[]\oplus U_{2r}([1])\ ,

(4.20)

where the Chebyshev polynomial
U_{d}
was defined in Eq. (
2.26
). This identity is statisfied by our expression (
2.2
) for
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
: in fact, this was an important hint for guessing that expression in the first place.

The algebraic definition of the representations
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
of the group
O(n)
that appear in the space of states also allows us to rederive their invariances under shifts (
2.23
) and reflection (
2.21
) of the second index. The invariance under shifts is now due to the fact that the representation
W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}
of the algebra
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
only depends on
s
via
e^{\pi is}
. The invariance under reflection is due to the automorphism of the diagram algebras that acts on the Brauer generators as
e_{i},p_{i}\to e_{L-i},p_{L-i}
. Under this automorphism, the representation
B^{(L)}_{\lambda}
of the Brauer algebra is invariant, but representations of the unoriented Jones–Temperley–Lieb algebra change as
W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}\to W^{(L)}_{(r,-s)}
. It follows that the coefficients of the decomposition (
4.15
) obey

\displaystyle c^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}=c^{\lambda}_{(r,-s)}\ .

(4.21)

Finally, let us discuss the tensor products of the representations
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
(
2.36
). Already in the case
\Lambda_{(\frac{1}{2},0)}\otimes\Lambda_{(1,0)}=[1]\otimes[2]=[3]+[21]+[1]
, we see that this set of representations is not closed under tensor products. While we may interpret
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
as irreducible representations of the commutant
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}^{*}(n)
, we can therefore not directly interpret this commutant as a symmetry algebra of the
O(n)
model.

4.2
Branching rules
\mathcal{B}_{L}(n)\downarrow u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)

We have reduced the determination of the
O(n)
representations
\Lambda_{(r,s)}
to the algebraic problem of decomposing representations of the Brauer algebra into representations of the unoriented Jones–Temperley–Lieb algebra. Let us now solve this problem.

Brauer–Specht modules of
\mathcal{B}_{L}(n)

The irreducible representation
B^{(L)}_{\lambda}
of the Brauer algebra is called a Brauer–Specht module. To build a basis, we start with all perfect matchings
b_{i}
from
L
lower sites to
|\lambda|
upper sites, such that each upper site is matched with a lower site, modulo permutations of the upper sites. In the case

{{}}{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 59.06 29.53 M 61.82 29.53 C 61.82 31.06 60.58 32.29 59.06 32.29 C 57.53 32.29 56.29 31.06 56.29 29.53 C 56.29 28 57.53 26.76 59.06 26.76 C 60.58 26.76 61.82 28 61.82 29.53 Z M 59.06 29.53}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 0 C 39.37 11.51 39.37 18.01 39.37 29.53}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
;
{{}}{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 59.06 0 C 59.06 11.51 59.06 18.01 59.06 29.53}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
;
{{}}{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 19.69 0 C 34.49 17.64 63.94 17.64 78.74 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
b_{1}&b_{2}&b_{3}&b_{4}&b_{5}&b_{6}\end{array}

The vector space
\mathbb{C}\{b_{i}\}
is a representation of the Brauer algebra
\mathcal{B}_{L}(n)
acting from below, and of the symmetric group
S_{|\lambda|}
acting from above, and we have

\displaystyle B^{(L)}_{\lambda}=\mathbb{C}\{b_{i}\}\otimes_{S_{|\lambda|}}\lambda\ ,

(4.24)

where the partition
\lambda
now stands for a Specht module of
S_{|\lambda|}
. In our example, that Specht module is one-dimensional since
\lambda\in\{[2],[11]\}
, and
\{b_{i}\}
may be considered as a basis of
B^{(4)}_{\lambda}
itself.

As a warm-up, let us decompose
B^{(4)}_{[2]}
and
B^{(4)}_{[11]}
into irreducible representations of the Temperley–Lieb algebra, which are described before Eq. (
A.10
). The diagrams
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}
do not involve any crossings, so they can be straightforwardly identified with elements of a representation of
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{4}(n)
. The irreducible representation that is generated by diagrams with
2r
upper sites is called
W^{(4)}_{r}
, so in our case the relevant representation is
W^{(4)}_{1}
.
Since our three diagrams are permuted by the action of
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
, they all belong to one copy of
W^{(4)}_{1}
, which is indeed three-dimensional according to Eq. (
A.10
). Then, notice that the action of all three Temperley–Lieb generators on
b_{4},b_{5},b_{6}
only give
b_{1},b_{2},b_{3}
: this implies that there is a three-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{C}\{b_{i}\}
that is annihilated by
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{4}(n)
. The one-dimensional representation
W^{(L)}_{\frac{L}{2}}
is generated by the trivial diagram with
L
lower and
L
upper site; the action of any Temperley–Lieb generator yields a diagram where two upper sites are matched, which is identified with zero.
Being annihilated by
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
is therefore the defining feature of
W^{(L)}_{\frac{L}{2}}
, and we deduce

\displaystyle B^{(4)}_{[2]}\underset{\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{4}(n)}{=}B^{(4)}_{[11]}\underset{\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{4}(n)}{=}W^{(4)}_{1}\oplus 3W^{(4)}_{2}\quad\iff\quad c^{[2]}_{1}=c^{[11]}_{1}=1\ ,\ c^{[2]}_{2}=c^{[11]}_{2}=3\ ,

(4.25)

which agrees with the general formula (
4.10
).

Diagrammatic and combinatorial subproblems

Let us reduce our problem to a subproblem of enumerating certain diagrams, and a combinatorial subproblem of enumerating Young tableaux.

To begin with, since the coefficient
c^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}
in the branching rule (
4.15
) is
L
-independent, we may choose the lowest value
L=2r
such that this coefficient appears. This reduces the problem to determining the contributions of representations of the type
W^{(L)}_{(\frac{L}{2},s)}
in
B^{(L)}_{\lambda}
. From their diagrammatic construction, these representations are annihilated by the
L
generators
e_{1},e_{2},\dots,e_{L}
of
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
. Since the image of the action of these generators is made of diagrams where at least two neighbouring bottom sites are connected, our representations may be built from diagrams where no two neighbouring bottom sites are connected. Let
\{b_{i}\}
be the set of perfect matchings from
L
bottom sites to
|\lambda|
top sites such that no two neighbouring bottom sites are connected, and no two top sites are connected, modulo permutations of the top sites: in the case
L=6,|\lambda|=4
we have
9
such diagrams:

(4.26a)

(4.26b)

These diagrams are split into two orbits of lengths
6,3
of the action of the generator
u
of
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{6}(n)
, which cyclically permutes the bottom sites. In general, let us call
\Omega^{(L)}_{|\lambda|}
the tuple of these orbit lengths, in our example
\Omega^{(6)}_{4}=(6,3)
.
Using Eq. (
4.19
), we may write the number of diagrams as

\displaystyle\sum_{\omega\in\Omega^{(L)}_{\ell}}\omega\underset{|\lambda|=\ell}{=}\frac{1}{f_{\lambda}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\frac{2}{L}\mathbb{Z}\\
-1<s\leq 1\end{subarray}}c^{\lambda}_{(\frac{L}{2},s)}=c^{\ell}_{\frac{L}{2}}-\delta_{L\geq 3}c^{\ell}_{\frac{L}{2}-1}\ ,

(4.27)

where the coefficients
c^{\ell}_{r}
are explicitly given in Eq. (
4.10
).
Our diagrammatic subproblem consists in determining
\Omega^{(L)}_{\ell}
.

Let us focus on one orbit of length
\omega
: then
u^{\omega}
induces a cyclic permutation
\mu
of the
|\lambda|
upper sites, such that
\mu^{\frac{L}{\omega}}=1
. This implies
\omega|\lambda|\in L\mathbb{N}
. Our combinatorial subproblem is to find the eigenvalues of
\mu
in the Specht module
\lambda
of the permutation group
S_{|\lambda|}
. Strictly speaking, this is an algebraic problem, which amounts to decomposing
\lambda
into representations of a cyclic subgroup of
S_{|\lambda|}
. However, this problem has a known combinatorial solution
[
33
]
:

\displaystyle\lambda\underset{\mathbb{Z}_{m}}{=}\bigoplus_{T\in T_{\lambda}}Z_{\frac{2}{m}\operatorname{ind}(T)}^{(m)}\ ,

(4.28)

where
Z^{(m)}_{s}
is the (one-dimensional) irreducible representation of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
where the generator of
\mathbb{Z}_{m}
has the eigenvalue
e^{\pi is}
,
T_{\lambda}
is the set of standard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda
(so that
|T_{\lambda}|=f_{\lambda}
), and the major index
\operatorname{ind}(T)\in\mathbb{Z}_{|\lambda|}
of a tableau is the sum of the descents, i.e. the sum of the numbers
k
in
T
such that
k+1
appears in a row strictly below
k
.

Branching rules

According to Eq. (
4.18
), the subrepresentations
W^{(L)}_{(\frac{L}{2},s)}
of
B^{(L)}_{\lambda}
are characterized by the eigenvalues of
u
.
We claim that these eigenvalues are the
\omega
-th roots of the eigenvalues of the permutations that are induced by elements of the type
u^{\omega}
. (See
[
34
]
(Appendix A.4) for an explanation of this point in the language of transfer matrices.)
The resulting branching coefficients are

\displaystyle\boxed{c^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}=\sum_{T\in T_{\lambda}}\sum_{\omega\in\Omega^{(2r)}_{|\lambda|}}\delta_{e^{\pi i\omega\left(s-\frac{\operatorname{ind}(T)}{r}\right)},1}}\ ,

(4.29)

and therefore the branching rules

\displaystyle\boxed{B^{(L)}_{\lambda}\underset{u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)}{=}\bigoplus_{r=\frac{|\lambda|}{2}}^{\frac{L}{2}}\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega^{(2r)}_{|\lambda|}}\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\omega}}\bigoplus_{T\in T_{\lambda}}W^{(L)}_{(r,\frac{2k}{\omega}+\frac{\operatorname{ind}(T)}{r})}}\ .

(4.30)

Let us examine a few special cases. First we focus on maximal representations, i.e. representations
B^{(L)}_{\lambda}
with
|\lambda|=L
. In this case, there is only one relevant diagram, which is trivial:

(4.31)

This implies
\Omega^{(L)}_{L}=(1)
, so the sum over
k
becomes trivial, and we end up with the purely combinatorial formula

\displaystyle B^{(L)}_{\lambda}\underset{u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)}{\overset{|\lambda|=L}{=}}\bigoplus_{T\in T_{\lambda}}W^{(L)}_{(\frac{L}{2},\frac{2}{L}\operatorname{ind}(T))}\ .

(4.32)

This is equivalent to the branching rule
S_{L}\downarrow\mathbb{Z}_{L}
(
4.28
).

Another interesting special case is when
\lambda=[|\lambda|]
is the trivial representation of
S_{|\lambda|}
. In this case,
T_{\lambda}
contains a unique tableau whose index is
0
, and we end up with the purely diagrammatic formula

\displaystyle B^{(L)}_{\lambda}\underset{u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)}{\overset{\lambda=[|\lambda|]}{=}}\bigoplus_{r=\frac{|\lambda|}{2}}^{\frac{L}{2}}\bigoplus_{\omega\in\Omega^{(2r)}_{|\lambda|}}\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\omega}}W^{(L)}_{(r,\frac{2k}{\omega})}\ .

(4.33)

A purely diagrammatic formula is also obtained if
\mathrm{gcd}(2r,|\lambda|)=1
, which can only happen if
2r
is odd. In this case,
\Omega^{(2r)}_{|\lambda|}
is a number of copies of the orbit length
2r
– smaller orbits cannot occur. Then
s
no longer depends on
\operatorname{ind}(T)
, and we have

\displaystyle B^{(L)}_{\lambda}\underset{u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)}{\overset{\mathrm{gcd}(2r,|\lambda|)=1}{=}}f_{\lambda}\bigoplus_{r=\frac{|\lambda|}{2}}^{\frac{L}{2}}\left|\Omega^{(2r)}_{|\lambda|}\right|\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{2r}}W^{(L)}_{(r,\frac{k}{r})}\ .

(4.34)

In other words,
c^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}=f_{\lambda}\left|\Omega^{(2r)}_{|\lambda|}\right|
is
s
-independent in this case.

Examples

Let us solve the diagrammatic subproblem with
6
bottom sites and
0
top site. We find
c^{0}_{3}-c^{0}_{2}=4
diagrams:

(4.35)

The action of
u
divides these diagrams into two orbits of lengths
3
and
1
, so that
\Omega^{(6)}_{0}=(3,1)
. The corresponding
u
-eigenvalues are
1,1,e^{\pm\frac{2\pi i}{3}}
, leading to

\displaystyle c^{[]}_{(3,0)}=2\quad,\quad c^{[]}_{(3,\pm\frac{1}{3})}=c^{[]}_{(3,1)}=0\quad,\quad c^{[]}_{(3,\pm\frac{2}{3})}=1\ .

(4.36)

In the case of
8
bottom sites and
0
top site, we find
c^{0}_{4}-c^{0}_{3}=31
diagrams that belong to
7
different
u
-orbits. Let us give one representative of each orbit, while indicating the orbit lengths:

\displaystyle 8:

(4.37a)

\displaystyle 4:

(4.37b)

\displaystyle 2:

(4.37c)

\displaystyle 1:

(4.37d)

Therefore, we have
\Omega_{0}^{(8)}=(8,8,4,4,4,2,1)
, which leads to

\displaystyle c^{[]}_{(4,0)}=7\quad,\quad c^{[]}_{(4,\pm\frac{1}{4})}=c^{[]}_{(4,\pm\frac{3}{4})}=2\quad,\quad c^{[]}_{(4,\pm\frac{1}{2})}=5\quad,\quad c^{[]}_{(4,1)}=6\ .

(4.38)

Let us now consider the case with
6
bottom sites and
4
top sites. We already used the determination of
\Omega^{(6)}_{4}=(6,3)
for illustrating the definition of
\Omega^{(L)}_{\ell}
, see the diagrams (
4.26
). Let us focus on the combinatorial subproblem. The five Young diagrams of size
4
, together with the sets
\{\operatorname{ind}(T)|T\in T_{\lambda}\}
of major indices of the corresponding Young tableaux, are:

\displaystyle[4]:\{0\}\quad,\quad[31]:\{1,2,3\}\quad,\quad[22]:\{0,2\}\quad,\quad[211]:\{0,1,3\}\quad,\quad[1111]:\{2\}\ .

(4.39)

The coefficient
c^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}
depends on
\operatorname{ind}(T)
only if
\omega<2r
, i.e. in our example if
\omega=3
. In this case, it depends only on
\operatorname{ind}(T)\bmod 2
. This implies
c^{[22]}_{(3,s)}=2c^{[4]}_{(3,s)}=2c^{[1111]}_{(3,s)}
, since
f_{[22]}=|T_{[22]}|=2
whereas
f_{[4]}=f_{[1111]}=1
. In particular,

\displaystyle c^{[4]}_{(3,0)}=c^{[4]}_{(3,\pm\frac{2}{3})}=2\quad,\quad c^{[4]}_{(3,1)}=c^{[4]}_{(3,\pm\frac{1}{3})}=1\ .

(4.40)

The cases where the dependence on
\operatorname{ind}(T)
plays a role are
c^{[31]}_{(3,s)}=c^{[211]}_{(3,s)}
. In these cases, the odd major indices
1,3
shift the values of
s
by one unit. Therefore, to compute
c^{[31]}_{(3,s)}
, we cannot simply multiply
c^{[4]}_{(3,s)}
by
f_{[31]}=3
. Instead, we obtain

\displaystyle c^{[31]}_{(3,0)}=c^{[31]}_{(3,\pm\frac{2}{3})}=4\quad,\quad c^{[31]}_{(3,1)}=c^{[31]}_{(3,\pm\frac{1}{3})}=5\ .

(4.41)

A longer list of
\mathcal{B}_{L}(n)\downarrow u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{L}(n)
branching rules, expressing
B^{(L)}_{\lambda}
in terms of
W^{(L)}_{(r,s)}
for any
|\lambda|\leq 4
and
r\leq 5
, can be found in Appendix
B.1
.

5
Algebraic approach to the
Q
-state Potts model

The space of states of the
Q
-state Potts model on a lattice with
L
sites can be constructed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\text{Potts}}_{L}=\Big([1]\oplus[]\Big)^{\otimes L}\ .

(5.1)

On each site, we have a variable that belongs to the natural
Q
-dimensional representation of the symmetric group
S_{Q}
. This representation is a sum of two irreducible representations, called the trivial and standard representations. In the notations of Section
3.1
, these representations correspond to the Young diagrams
[]
and
[1]
respectively.

5.1
Action of diagram algebras on the space of states

We will now introduce the diagram algebras that act on the space of states of the
Q
-state Potts model. We will focus on the algebraic properties of these algebras and their representations: for more details, including the eponymous diagrams, see Appendix
A
.

The relevant diagram algebras

The algebra of the linear maps on
\mathcal{S}^{\text{Potts}}_{L}
that commute with
S_{Q}
is the partition algebra,

\displaystyle S_{Q}^{*}=\mathcal{P}_{L}(Q)\ .

(5.2)

The partition algebra is generated by the permutations
p_{i}
with
i=1,\dots,L-1
, together with generators
s_{i}
with
i=1,\dots,L
and
s_{i+\frac{1}{2}}
with
i=1,\dots,L-1
. These generators act on one or two factors of
([1]+[])^{\otimes L}
, and for
Q
integer they can be defined as
[
35
]

\displaystyle s_{i}:

\displaystyle\quad\begin{array}[]{lll}([1]+[])_{i}&\to&([1]+[])_{i}\\
w_{k}&\mapsto&\sum_{\ell=1}^{Q}w_{\ell}\end{array}\ ,

\displaystyle s_{i+\frac{1}{2}}:

\displaystyle\quad\begin{array}[]{lll}([1]+[])_{i}\otimes([1]+[])_{i+1}&\to&([1]+[])_{i}\otimes([1]+[])_{i+1}\\
w_{k}\otimes w_{\ell}&\mapsto&\delta_{k\ell}w_{k}\otimes w_{k}\end{array}\ ,

where
(w_{k})_{k}
is a basis of
[1]+[]
such that
S_{Q}
acts by permuting the indices
k
.
As a diagram algebra, the partition algebra exists for any
Q\in\mathbb{C}
, and the generators
p_{i},s_{i},s_{i+\frac{1}{2}}
are described by the diagrams (
A.3
).

The partition algebra includes the permutation group
S_{L}
, and ignores the geometry of the problem. If the
L
spins are arrayed in a chain, we can no longer permute them. The symmetries of the system must then be described by a smaller algebra, which also takes into account the boundary conditions. For an open chain with free boundary conditions, the relevant algebra is obtained by removing the permutation generators
p_{i}
. This leads to the Temperley–Lieb algebra
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
, via the map
s_{i}\mapsto e_{2i-1}
for
i=1,\frac{3}{2},\dots,L-\frac{1}{2},L
.

For a closed chain with periodic boundary conditions, we should furthermore add two generators: the generator
s_{L+\frac{1}{2}}\mapsto e_{2L}
, and the generator
u^{2}
which translates all the sites by two units in
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
. (This corresponds to translations by one unit in the original chain with
L
sites.)
In order to describe clusters rather than loops, we need to make a couple extra modifications, see Appendix
A.2
.
Let us call the resulting algebra the Potts–Temperley–Lieb algebra
P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
.

Representations of the partition algebra

By Schur–Weyl duality, our space of states decomposes into irreducible representations of
\mathcal{P}_{L}(Q)\times S_{Q}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\text{Potts}}_{L}\underset{\mathcal{P}_{L}(Q)\times S_{Q}}{=}\bigoplus_{|\lambda|\leq L}P_{\lambda}^{(L)}\otimes\lambda\ ,

(5.7)

where the sum is over partitions
\lambda
, which we identify with irreducible representations of
S_{Q}
at generic
Q
. The irreducible finite-dimensional representations
P_{\lambda}^{(L)}
of the partition algebra are also labelled by partitions: we will say more on their structures when it comes to computing branching rules in Section
5.2
. For the moment, let us point out that their dimensions appear as coefficients in the decomposition of
\mathcal{S}^{\text{Potts}}_{L}=([1]\oplus[])^{\otimes L}
into irreducible representations of
S_{Q}
,

\displaystyle\Big([1]\oplus[]\Big)^{\otimes L}\underset{S_{Q}}{=}\bigoplus_{|\lambda|\leq L}\left(\dim P_{\lambda}^{(L)}\right)\lambda\ .

(5.8)

For example,
([1]\oplus[])^{\otimes 2}=[2]+[11]+3[1]+2[]
tells us that
\dim P^{(2)}_{[1]}=3
.

Representations of the Temperley–Lieb algebra

From Section
4.1
, we remember that the standard modules
W^{(2L)}_{r}
of the Temperley–Lieb algebra
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
are labelled by integers
r=0,1,\dots,L
. By analogy with the case of the Brauer algebra (
4.8
), we expect branching rules of the type

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}^{(L)}\underset{\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})}{=}f_{\lambda}\bigoplus_{r=|\lambda|}^{L}d_{r}^{|\lambda|}W_{r}^{(2L)}\ ,

(5.9)

with some coefficients
d^{|\lambda|}_{r}\in\mathbb{N}
that depend neither on
Q
nor on
L
, and a prefactor
f_{\lambda}
given by the hook length formula (
4.9
). Inserting this into the decomposition (
5.7
) of the space of states, we obtain

\displaystyle\boxed{\mathcal{S}^{\text{Potts}}_{L}\underset{\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})\times S_{Q}}{=}\bigoplus_{r=0}^{L}W_{r}^{(2L)}\otimes\Xi_{r}}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\boxed{\Xi_{r}=\bigoplus_{|\lambda|\leq r}f_{\lambda}d_{r}^{|\lambda|}\lambda}\ .

(5.10)

We can recursively deduce the representations
\Xi_{r}
, by writing
\mathcal{S}^{\text{Potts}}_{L}\underset{S_{Q}}{=}\bigoplus_{r=0}^{L}\left(\dim W_{r}^{(2L)}\right)\Xi_{r}
and using the dimensions (
A.10
) of Temperley–Lieb representations. The first few examples are

\displaystyle\Xi_{0}

\displaystyle=[]\ ,

(5.11)

\displaystyle\Xi_{1}

\displaystyle=[1]\ ,

(5.12)

\displaystyle\Xi_{2}

\displaystyle=[2]+[11]\ ,

(5.13)

\displaystyle\Xi_{3}

\displaystyle=[3]+2[21]+[111]+[2]+[11]+[1]\ .

(5.14)

We can also deduce the recursion relation

\displaystyle[1]\otimes\Xi_{r}=\Xi_{r-1}\oplus\Xi_{r}\oplus\Xi_{r+1}\ .

(5.15)

Using the associativity of the tensor product, this implies

\displaystyle\Xi_{r_{1}}\otimes\Xi_{r_{2}}=\bigoplus_{r=|r_{1}-r_{2}|}^{r_{1}+r_{2}}\Xi_{r}\ .

(5.16)

We may interpret
\Xi_{r}
as an irreducible representation of the commutant
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}^{*}(\sqrt{Q})
.

Representations of the Potts–Temperley–Lieb algebra

For
r\geq 2
, the irreducible representations of the Potts–Temperley–Lieb algebra
P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
simply coincide with irreducible representations
W_{(r,s)}^{(2L)}
of the unoriented algebra
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
, which we described in Eq. (
A.29
). However, in the unoriented case, the pseudo-momentum
s
was defined modulo
2
because the generator
u
of
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
had eigenvalues
e^{\pi is}
. In the case of
P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
, only
u^{2}
exists, and
s
is now defined modulo integers. In other words, the following inequivalent representations of
u\mathcal{J\!T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
become equivalent as representations of
P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
:

\displaystyle W_{(r,s)}^{(2L)}\underset{P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})}{\simeq}W_{(r,s+1)}^{(2L)}\ .

(5.17)

Therefore, we assume
s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\cap(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
for representations of
P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
. As reviewed in Appendix
A.2
,
P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
has no representation with
r=1
, and two representations with
r=0
, which we call
W^{(2L)}_{(0,0)}
and
W^{(2L)}_{(0,\frac{1}{2})}
.
We expect that representations of the partition algebra decompose as

\displaystyle\boxed{P^{(L)}_{\lambda}\underset{P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})}{=}\delta_{\lambda,[]}W^{(2L)}_{(0,0)}\oplus\delta_{\lambda,[1]}W^{(2L)}_{(0,\frac{1}{2})}\oplus\bigoplus_{r=\max(2,|\lambda|)}^{L}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\\
-\frac{1}{2}<s\leq\frac{1}{2}\end{subarray}}d^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}W^{(2L)}_{(r,s)}}\ ,

(5.18)

where the coefficients
d^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}\in\mathbb{N}
depend neither on
Q
nor on
L
. We will explain how to compute them in Section
5.2
. For the moment, let us proceed with decomposing the space of states. Inserting our decomposition of
P^{(L)}_{\lambda}
into Eq. (
5.7
), we find

\boxed{\mathcal{S}_{L}^{\text{Potts}}\underset{P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})\times S_{Q}}{=}W^{(2L)}_{(0,0)}\otimes[]\oplus W^{(2L)}_{(0,\frac{1}{2})}\otimes[1]\oplus\bigoplus_{r=2}^{L}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\\
-\frac{1}{2}<s\leq\frac{1}{2}\end{subarray}}W^{(2L)}_{(r,s)}\otimes\Xi_{(r,s)}}\\
\text{with}\qquad\boxed{\Xi_{(r,s)}=\bigoplus_{|\lambda|\leq r}d^{\lambda}_{(r,s)}\lambda}\ .

(5.19)

The representation
\Xi_{(r,s)}
of
S_{Q}
that we just defined should coincide with the representation
\Xi_{(r,s)}
that appeared in the spectrum (
3.10
) of the Potts CFT. Moreover, the large
L
limit of the representation
W^{(2L)}_{(r,s)}
should coincide with a representation of the interchiral algebra,

\displaystyle\lim_{\text{critical}}W^{(2L)}_{(r,s)}={\mathchoice{\widetilde{\hbox{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\textstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}{\widetilde{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}$}}}}_{(r,s)}\ .

(5.20)

We can obtain some nontrivial information on
\Xi_{(r,s)}
by comparing our decomposition (
5.19
) of the space of states with the decomposition (
5.10
) into representions of the Temperley–Lieb algebra. Using the relations (
A.29
) and (
A.30
) between modules of
P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
and
\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\in\frac{1}{r}\mathbb{Z}\\
-\frac{1}{2}<s\leq\frac{1}{2}\end{subarray}}\Xi_{(r,s)}=\Xi_{r}-\delta_{r\geq 1}\Xi_{r-1}+(-1)^{r}[1]\ .

(5.21)

The algebraic definition of the representations
\Xi_{(r,s)}
of the group
S_{Q}
that appear in the space of states also allows us to rederive their invariances under shifts (
3.13
) and reflection (
2.21
), by the same arguments as in the
O(n)
model.

Unlike the representations
\Xi_{r}
with their tensor products (
5.16
), the representations
\Xi_{(r,s)}
(
3.19
) do not close under tensor products. While we may interpret
\Xi_{(r,s)}
as irreducible representations of the commutant
P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}^{*}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})
, we can therefore not interpret this commutant as a symmetry algebra of the
Q
-state Potts model.

5.2
Branching rules
\mathcal{P}_{L}(Q)\downarrow P\!\mathcal{T\!\!L}_{2L}(\sqrt{Q})

We have reduced the determination of the
S_{Q}
representations
\Xi_{(r,s)}
to the algebraic problem of decomposing representations of the partition algebra into representations of the Potts–Temperley–Lieb algebra. Let us now solve this problem.

Diagrammatic and combinatorial subproblems

Let us describe the structure of irreducible representation
P_{\lambda}^{(L)}
of the partition algebra. To build a basis, we start with the set of all partitions of
L
lower sites and
|\lambda|
upper sites, such that each upper site belongs to a subset that contains no other upper site, and at least one lower site. To draw such diagrams, we draw lines between elements of a given subset, without necessarily drawing all possible lines:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 15.75 0 C 15.75 9.21 15.75 14.41 15.75 23.62}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
;
{{}}{}{{}}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}
{{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}}{{{}}{{}}}{{}}{}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 15.75 0 C 15.75 6.14 31.5 6.14 31.5 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
;
